#10833. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

#10832. Epic reveals Fortnite Pirate Shop Token odds after days of player backlash

Fortnite players are calling on Epic Games to reveal the odds of finding Pirate Shop Access after days of failed chest hunts, conflicting community estimates, and claims that a server-side system called “Clover” controls the reward.

Pirate Shop Access was added with the Treasure Hunters mini-season on November 1. Players who find it receive two activations, each opening a 14-day window during which eligible Item Shop cosmetics can be claimed without spending V-Bucks. Real-money offers, gifting, and Battle Pass rewards are excluded.

Epic has not published a percentage explaining how often Pirate Shop Access can appear. Fortnite has only said that the reward works like a normal Battle Royale chest and that every player has an equal opportunity to find it. That statement does not explain whether the chance is one in 100, one in 10,000, or the rumored 1/700,000.

Hey @Pirat_Nation – Buying an account or using fake tools is a scam. This is just like any of the chests in Battle Royale, so every player has an equal opportunity to find Pirate Shop Access.

The lack of an official figure has allowed several different estimates to spread online, with no way for players to verify which, if any, is accurate. That has become especially frustrating for players who have spent hours opening chests without knowing whether they are experiencing normal bad luck or chasing a reward with an effectively unreachable chance.

Epic Games has now revealed the official odds of finding Pirate Shop Access after players spent days demanding answers.

In a Fortnite Status post on November 7, Epic said Pirate Shop Access has a 1 in 137,500 chance of appearing each time a player opens a chest in a normal match. The company said those rates have remained constant since the Treasure Hunters season began and do not change throughout the day, except during scheduled Treasure Hours.

During Treasure Hours, the Pirate Shop Access rate is boosted by 1.5x, with Epic warning that future events could offer even higher multipliers. Epic also said players cannot receive the same treasure twice, except for Gizmos.

Fortnite’s ultra-rare Pirate Coin hunt sparks paid carries, AFK tools, and a $10,000 eBay listing

Fortnite adds rare reward that makes entire Item Shop free for 14 days, but there’s limits

Epic Games gives update to Fortnite players claiming they still haven’t been paid

You asked for info on drop rates, so here’s how it works:The new treasure this season is found in chests just like other items. Treasure drop rates from chests are constant since season launch and don’t vary throughout the day, except during Treasure Hours. Other than Gizmos,…

The newly revealed rate confirms that the reward is extremely rare, but it does not guarantee a token after a certain number of chest openings. Each chest remains a separate chance, meaning players can still open thousands without receiving Pirate Shop Access.

Streamer Jynxzi has reportedly spent more than 75 hours searching for the token, while Stable Ronaldo claims he’s spent over 70 hours hunting. Both streamers have called on Epic to publish the odds.

The “Clover” claims have added another layer to the controversy. Fortnite leaker Egyptian_Leaker claimed Epic created a system that can adjust the token’s odds without exposing the numbers in the game files. Llamalaxy later claimed that the reward roll is handled by a live backend service when a chest is opened, which would explain why dataminers cannot find a fixed percentage in the client.

Those claims have not been independently confirmed by Epic.

Epic’s official Treasure Hunters announcement says Treasure Hours will feature boosted drops, but it does not specifically confirm whether Pirate Shop Access becomes more likely during those events.

Fortnite made a whole new dev system just to hide the Pirate Shop Token odds 😭– They named this system "Clover"– It ensures that they can modify the odds without the leakers knowing pic.twitter.com/61Il08YCv0

That leaves players with several unanswered questions. Are the odds fixed for the entire season? Are they the same for every player and chest type? Can Epic change them during the event? Do Treasure Hours affect the token at all?

Traditional gambling definitions generally involve chance, a prize, and something of value being risked. A federal definition of a bet or wager specifically excludes contests where participants risk only personal effort or internet access, although state laws differ.

Because Pirate Shop Access is earned by playing Fortnite and opening chests without paying V-Bucks for each attempt, the available facts are not enough to call the system illegal gambling.

The FTC has discussed both paid loot boxes and random rewards earned through gameplay. Its guidance focuses on whether odds or costs are presented accurately and without misleading players. The agency’s 2025 action involving Genshin Impact concerned paid loot boxes, virtual currency, and allegedly misleading odds.

Based on those public materials, the absence of a published Pirate Shop percentage alone does not establish an FTC violation. The key questions would be whether Epic made misleading claims about the reward, whether players were required or encouraged to spend money, and whether the odds changed without accurate disclosure.

Epic’s public response has addressed equal access and scams, but it has not provided the numerical odds players are asking for.

#10828. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10824. 7 Ways to Keep API Changes Safe as More Teams Join a Project

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A backend team renames a response field from name to displayName. Its tests pass, the deployment succeeds, and the API continues returning HTTP 200. Minutes later, a dashboard starts showing blank user labels.

The failure is easy to explain: the server changed a contract that another application depended on. Preventing it requires more than asking teams to communicate better. The dependency needs to become visible in documentation, tests, and deployment decisions.

This article follows that field rename through seven practical safeguards. The examples apply to frontend and backend teams working in the same office, across departments, or in different countries.

Start by documenting the existing response and the intended replacement.

The two objects may look equivalent to a person, but software reads specific keys. A client using user.name will not automatically discover displayName.

An OpenAPI description can document request and response shapes alongside status codes and other interface details. Keep it in version control and update it in the same change as the implementation.

Document meaning as well as structure. Does displayName contain user-supplied text? Can it be empty? Is it intended for display only? Must consumers avoid using it as an identifier?

SitePoint’s REST API best practices provides background on interface design. For an existing API, the immediate task is to identify the promises clients already rely on.

An endpoint can serve more than the application shown in the product demo. Consumers may include an administrative dashboard, a mobile app, scheduled exports, internal scripts, and external integrations.

The point is to discover different upgrade timelines. A web application may update quickly, while an installed mobile client remains active for months.

Inspect request telemetry where available, but do not treat a short period of inactivity as proof that a consumer is unused. A quarterly export will not appear in a one-week observation window.

Also record whether consumers reject unknown fields. Adding a response property is often easier to migrate than removing one, but strict decoders can make even additive changes incompatible.

For this example, a transitional response can expose both fields:

Generate both values from the same source so they cannot drift during the migration.

The fourth step needs evidence. The server usually cannot observe which property a client reads after receiving a response. Use consumer version information, published contracts, maintained integration records, and explicit confirmations where appropriate.

For external APIs, removal may require a new version or a documented deprecation process. Do not remove a field simply because the internal dashboard has migrated.

Consider rollback before deployment. If the consumer now requires the new field, rolling the server back to an older response can break it. Either preserve compatibility during rollback or give the consumer a deliberate transition strategy.

A temporary adapter can let a consumer accept both the old and transitional response formats. Keep it narrow and document when it can be removed.

Save the following runnable example as user-label.test.mjs:

Run it with node --test user-label.test.mjs.

The fallback policy is a product decision. For a navigation label, “Unnamed user” may be acceptable. For an operation that requires a verified identity, missing required data may need to stop the workflow.

These tests exercise the adapter only. They do not prove that the live API returns the expected payload or that the application calls the adapter. Add integration coverage at the boundary where the response enters the client.

For UI rendering, insert the returned label as text or use your framework’s normal escaping. The adapter is not an HTML sanitizer.

Several tools can contribute to the review, but their responsibilities differ.

A useful pipeline can lint the API description, compare it against the released baseline, run consumer tests, and verify provider behavior.

Store the baseline deliberately. Comparing two files from the same proposed change can miss a regression against the deployed contract.

SitePoint’s Postman workflow guide explains collections and request organization. Keep shared examples free of production credentials, and include failure responses alongside successful ones.

Mocks should include old, transitional, and new payloads during a migration. This gives frontend developers and designers a way to inspect loading states, missing data, and error handling before the provider rollout finishes.

As more contributors join a project, architectural decisions need to remain understandable outside the meeting where they were made.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

For a distributed project, the engineering requirement is concrete: a new contributor should be able to identify who owns an interface, which consumers depend on it, and how a change reaches production.

Use actual team names and version identifiers in a real repository. Link the note from the pull request and make unresolved decisions explicit.

A reviewer should not need to infer whether a field is already deprecated or whether the mobile team has been consulted. That context should remain available when colleagues work different schedules.

Define what success looks like before shipping. For this rename, useful checks might include schema-validation failures, client exceptions around user rendering, and unexpected increases in missing-name fallbacks.

Avoid putting names or entire response bodies into diagnostic logs. A template version, consumer version, error category, and correlation identifier may provide sufficient evidence.

Test combinations that can exist during deployment:

Do not assume every combination is supported forever. Define the supported window and its rollback implications.

Once the removal condition is satisfied, retire the old field and temporary adapter through reviewed changes. Update fixtures and documentation so they describe the current contract.

A safe API migration gives teams an explicit sequence, tests the compatibility assumptions, and preserves enough evidence to decide when the transition is complete. That is what allows contributors to work independently without surprising the applications that depend on them.

#10823. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

#10819. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10816. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

Materials provided by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10815. 7 Checks That Make Form Bugs Easier to Reproduce Across Teams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer reports that a form’s error message overlaps its submit button. The developer opens the same page and sees no problem. Both are using the same code, but one has a narrow viewport, a translated interface, and an unusually long validation message.

A screenshot records what appeared on screen. A reproducible example also preserves the inputs, responses, permissions, and environment that produced it. That distinction matters when people review work at different times or join a project without knowing its undocumented conventions.

This article uses a workspace-invitation form as an example. The form accepts an email address and a role, submits a request, and displays either a confirmation or an error. The seven checks below turn its awkward states into something developers and designers can inspect together.

“The form looks broken” leaves too much for the next person to discover.

A useful report specifies the starting state, action, observed result, and expected behavior:

Include the browser, application revision, and relevant account permissions. Explain whether “360 pixels” means the browser’s layout viewport or the dimensions of an exported screenshot.

Avoid using real customer addresses in shared fixtures. A synthetic address such as member@example.test is sufficient to exercise the interface.

This report gives a designer a clear layout requirement and gives a developer a precise state to recreate.

A form tested only with successful submissions is missing most of its interesting behavior.

Create a small set of fixtures that cover the product’s actual rules:

Keep fixture values deterministic. Random addresses or constantly changing timestamps make visual comparisons harder to interpret.

Record expected outcomes alongside the data. A response containing a message does not establish where the interface should display it, whether focus should move, or which fields should remain editable.

Treat those as product decisions and test them explicitly.

Some failures can be reproduced by passing props to a component. Others depend on the request lifecycle.

Storybook args can describe inputs for isolated component states. An error-state story might supply a message, current field values, and a pending flag to a presentational form component.

However, passing pending: true does not prove that the real application enters or exits that state correctly.

For network-dependent behavior, use Mock Service Worker or your test runner’s request interception to supply a controlled response. Preserve the distinction between these two checks:

Avoid mocking the same endpoint in several overlapping layers. When a response is unexpected, the team should be able to identify which fixture produced it.

Also keep at least some integration coverage against the real API. A frontend mock that returns an outdated error shape can make the interface look reliable while hiding a broken contract.

Open the failing state at the recorded viewport. Inspect the error container, its parent layout, and the submit button.

Look for constraints such as fixed heights, absolute positioning, hidden overflow, and a flex or grid item that refuses to shrink.

A possible layout for a message beside an action is:

This pattern allows the message column to shrink and stacks the elements at a narrow width. It is not a universal fix: choose a breakpoint and arrangement that suit the surrounding form.

If the error belongs to a particular field, placing it beside the submit button may be the wrong design regardless of how well it wraps.

SitePoint’s browser-based CSS debugging guide explains how to inspect applied rules and layout behavior. Its guide to troubleshooting CSS with Inspect Element provides additional background.

Use DevTools experiments to identify the cause, then make the lasting change in the source. Check a wide viewport afterward so a narrow-screen fix does not introduce a desktop regression.

A screenshot can show that an error is present. It cannot, by itself, prove that the form preserved the email address or prevented an unintended navigation.

The following Playwright example assumes an existing application route, configured baseURL, and accessible form labels. Adapt the endpoint, response schema, and selectors to your project.

The example expects a native select for the role and a named form landmark. A custom role picker requires different interactions.

The behavior assertions precede the visual comparison. The screenshot then helps reviewers spot overlap, clipping, or unintended spacing changes.

Playwright’s visual comparison documentation explains baseline screenshots. Generate and compare them in a consistent environment; operating-system and font differences can affect rendering.

Review the first baseline manually. A screenshot test can faithfully preserve a bug if the initial image is approved without inspection. Disabling animation for a snapshot also does not test the accessibility of motion itself.

As a project grows, a component’s behavior should remain discoverable without a private walkthrough.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

The practical requirement for distributed contributors is a review process that carries its own context. Attach the exact fixture, preview location, reproduction command, and expected behavior to the issue or pull request.

Keep the open decision separate from the implemented behavior. This prevents reviewers from assuming that a visual change has already resolved a product question.

Name the person or team responsible for the component, and identify where design decisions are recorded. A stable fixture is useful only if someone maintains it as the component evolves.

A form that no longer overlaps can still be difficult to use.

Verify visible labels, keyboard navigation, focus visibility, and the relationship between errors and their fields. For field-level errors, consider aria-invalid and aria-describedby where appropriate.

The alert in the test example assumes a newly displayed form-level error. Do not add several competing live regions or announce every keystroke. Check the resulting experience with assistive technology.

SitePoint’s accessible form layout guide is relevant background for the relationship between structure and usability.

A focused toolset can support the workflow:

Use the smallest set that answers the current question. Adding another tool is useful when it closes a testing gap, not merely because it produces another report.

A good handoff lets someone unfamiliar with the original conversation recreate the failure, understand the expected behavior, and verify the fix. When the fixture and regression check remain with the code, that work continues to help the next contributor.

#10814. New Microsoft patent could use AI to alter speech and lip movements in real time

Microsoft has filed a patent for AI tech that could change what people say during live broadcasts in near real time, then alter their lip movements to match.

The filing, published on October 29, describes a system that scans live video and audio, picks out words to change, and swaps them for new ones before viewers see the footage.

According to the filing, as detailed by Patentlyze, an AI agent identifies spoken words to modify based on the profile of the person watching, such as their age, location, and the languages they speak.

A generative AI system then replaces those words in the audio, smooths over the edits so they sound natural, and generates new video frames so the speaker’s mouth matches the new words.

The patent says this would happen in “near-real-time,” which Patentlyze says would mean a delay of a few seconds. The system could also run on phones, tablets, cameras and AR glasses.

The filing gives several examples of how it could be used, including cutting out filler words like “um,” replacing profanity or offensive language, and fixing slang, mumbled or mispronounced words.

Replacement words could even be pulled from an internet search, which Patentlyze says could allow for instant fact-checking.

A separate section covers translation, where words spoken in a language the viewer doesn’t understand are swapped for translated words, with the speaker’s lips redrawn to match.

Because edits are tied to each viewer’s profile, two people watching the same broadcast could hear different versions of what was said.

The patent also describes using AI to condense long recordings into shorter summary videos, generating new footage of the speakers saying a summarized script.

Microsoft patents system for AI helpers to finish games for you

#10810. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10809. Viral hawk that ate too many chickens is flying again after losing weight

A hawk that became too fat to fly after feasting on chickens and sleeping all day is finally able to fly again after losing weight.

The red-shouldered hawk (RSHA) was taken in by the Southwest Virginia Wildlife Center of Roanoke after being found grounded in a driveway.

Staff discovered she weighed more than 910 grams (2 lbs), having apparently spent weeks eating a neighbor’s free-range chickens before sleeping in nearby trees.

In an update shared on Facebook, the wildlife center revealed that the bird has lost approximately 130 grams (4.6 oz) in a little over a month thanks to a combination of diet and exercise.

“Update on the Queen Herself, Miss RSHA 26-2599. She’s flying again!” the center wrote.

“Now, with some extra weight off, she’s starting to fly once again!”

However, the hawk isn’t quite ready to return to the wild. Despite her progress, staff say she remains significantly overweight and can only manage a handful of laps around her flight enclosure before becoming exhausted.

“There’s still a long way to go until release, but we’re thrilled with the progress!” the center explained.

“She is still significantly overweight for an adult RSHA, and she can only do 4-5 laps in her flight pen before she’s exhausted (it’s a lot of bird to keep in the air, after all!)”

The bird previously attracted widespread attention after the wildlife center revealed that her weight had left her unable to fly, despite examinations finding no obvious injuries or other health problems.

While there’s still no confirmed date for her release, the center thanked everyone who had followed her recovery.

#10807. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Colin Klein and Andrew Barron. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10805. Building CRM Automations That Don't Break: APIs, Webhooks, and AI Validation

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Modern businesses rarely struggle with a shortage of software. The bigger problem is getting their existing systems to communicate properly.

A lead arrives through a website form, but the sales team doesn't receive a notification. Someone books an appointment, yet their CRM record isn't updated. A prospect replies to an automated message, but the follow-up sequence continues as though nothing happened.

These are integration problems rather than software problems.

Platforms such as GoHighLevel aim to solve them by combining contact management, messaging, appointment scheduling and workflow automation in one environment. However, the real value comes from how these components are configured and connected to other applications.

For developers, agencies and technical consultants, GoHighLevel provides an interesting example of how low-code automation and API-driven development can work together.

This guide explores the architecture behind an effective CRM automation system, how GoHighLevel's integrations operate, and where custom development makes sense. The patterns below — data normalisation, idempotent event handling, queue-based processing and validated AI outputs — apply to any CRM; GoHighLevel is used here as the running example because it combines native workflows with a public API and webhooks in one platform.

It's tempting to think of automation as a collection of simple rules.

When someone submits a form, send an email. When a customer books a meeting, update their status. When a payment arrives, trigger a notification.

Individually, these are straightforward tasks.

Problems emerge when different workflows start interacting.

Consider a business that generates leads through several channels:

Referral partners and external lead providers

Each channel introduces different information, sometimes in different formats.

One source might supply a full name, phone number and email address. Another provides only a phone number. A third might include campaign information, product preferences or qualification answers.

Without a consistent data structure, CRM records become fragmented. Duplicate contacts appear, important information disappears and automations begin acting on incomplete data.

A well-designed system needs to address these problems before adding more workflow complexity.

GoHighLevel combines native workflow tools with an API that allows developers to integrate external services.

Its core components include contact records, conversations, calendars, opportunities, pipelines and workflow automation.

Developers can interact with supported resources through the platform's REST API, while webhooks provide a mechanism for reacting to events.

Information enters the system through forms, funnels, integrations, inbound communication or API requests.

At this stage, the priority is collecting reliable information and associating it with the correct contact.

Once information has been received, workflows can evaluate conditions and initiate actions.

For example, a newly created lead might trigger an internal notification, a qualification sequence or an appointment invitation.

Some business processes require functionality beyond the CRM's native capabilities.

An organisation might need to synchronise an accounting platform, update an external database or pass information into a proprietary application.

This is where APIs, webhooks and middleware become especially useful.

The important architectural decision is determining which tasks belong inside the CRM and which should be handled externally.

GoHighLevel's developer platform offers REST endpoints for supported operations involving contacts, conversations, calendars, payments and other CRM resources.

Its API uses JSON and supports token-based authentication, including OAuth 2.0 for applications requiring delegated access.

For developers creating an internal integration, a Private Integration Token may be appropriate. Public applications serving multiple accounts generally require a different authentication architecture.

A basic API request follows a familiar pattern:

This example illustrates the structure of a contact-creation request. Production implementations should validate the current endpoint schema, manage tokens securely, handle retries and consider using an upsert operation when duplicate prevention is required.

Two production details deserve attention up front. First, treat a 429 rate-limit response as a normal control signal, not an error: back off exponentially, respect any retry-after value, and never retry in a tight loop. Second, prefer upserts keyed on a stable identifier (a normalised phone number or email address) over blind creates, so a retried request cannot spawn a duplicate contact.

The same principles apply when synchronising information from external applications.

For example, an insurance business might receive enquiries through a specialist quoting system. Rather than manually transferring those records, an integration could normalise the information and send it to the CRM.

The key is to treat the API as part of a broader data architecture, not merely as another way to trigger messages.

APIs are useful when an application needs to retrieve or modify information.

Webhooks address a different requirement: knowing when something has happened.

A webhook-driven architecture might look like this:

A configured webhook sends event data to an external endpoint.

The receiving application verifies and processes the event.

This is particularly useful when connecting CRM activity to internal dashboards, fulfilment systems or custom databases.

However, webhook integrations require careful handling.

A publicly accessible webhook endpoint shouldn't automatically trust every request it receives.

Where the platform provides signature verification, developers should validate the signature using the documented method before processing the payload. In practice this means computing an HMAC over the raw request body with the shared signing secret and comparing it to the signature header in constant time — never parsing the body first and re-serialising it, which can change the bytes the signature was computed over.

If an appointment event triggers an invoice, processing the same event twice could create duplicate invoices.

An idempotent handler should recognise an event that has already been processed and avoid repeating side effects. The reliable way to do this is to store each event's unique ID (or a hash of its payload) in a small processed-events table and check it before running any side effect — a duplicate delivery then becomes a cheap no-op instead of a second invoice.

Long-running operations shouldn't necessarily execute inside the initial webhook request.

A more resilient approach is to validate the event, record it, acknowledge receipt and place any substantial work on a processing queue. Return a 2xx response within a second or two so the sender does not retry, then let a worker handle the slow steps. Failed jobs should retry with exponential backoff and, after a set number of attempts, land in a dead-letter queue where a human can inspect them instead of being retried forever.

This helps prevent slow third-party services from disrupting event handling.

Consider a company receiving enquiries through paid advertising, website forms and telephone calls.

The objective is simple: respond quickly, qualify potential customers and book appointments.

A practical CRM workflow could follow several stages.

Incoming data should be converted into a consistent format.

Phone numbers should be standardised, source information should be preserved, and duplicate detection should be considered before creating another contact.

It's also important to retain communication preferences and the relevant consent information.

Not every lead should be distributed randomly.

Assignment logic might consider geographical territory, product category, team availability or an existing customer relationship.

For more complicated organisations, routing decisions may need to be calculated in an external service.

Once the lead has been assigned, the system can initiate an appropriate follow-up.

This might involve notifying a sales representative, sending a permitted acknowledgement or offering an appointment booking link.

The workflow needs to respond to changing circumstances.

If the prospect books an appointment, unnecessary booking reminders should stop.

If a sales representative takes ownership, automated outreach may need to pause.

If the prospect opts out, the system must respect that preference across relevant communication channels.

Finally, the system should record what happened.

Useful measurements include lead response time, appointment booking rate, appointment attendance and conversion rate.

These metrics make it possible to distinguish a workflow that simply runs from one that improves business operations.

Artificial intelligence introduces additional possibilities, particularly around lead qualification, conversation handling and information extraction.

An AI-powered system might analyse an incoming enquiry, identify the customer's requirements and suggest an appropriate next action.

It could also summarise a conversation for a sales representative or help classify enquiries into different service categories.

However, introducing an AI model creates new engineering considerations.

Allowing an AI model to generate unrestricted text is rarely sufficient when that output controls business processes.

A more predictable approach is to require structured responses and validate them before updating CRM records.

The application should check that the response matches the expected schema before performing any action.

Some actions are better handled by deterministic rules.

Communication consent, payment processing, account permissions and important customer record changes shouldn't depend solely on a language model's interpretation.

AI can help classify or summarise information, but critical operations should remain subject to validated rules and appropriate human oversight.

AI assistants can reduce repetitive work, but they should also recognise their limitations.

A prospect asking an unusual technical question or requesting a customised proposal may need to speak with a person.

The best automation systems provide clear escalation paths rather than trying to automate every possible interaction.

GoHighLevel's visual workflow builder makes many routine automations accessible to non-developers.

A basic appointment reminder or lead notification may require little custom development.

The situation changes when several systems need to work together.

For example, a business might need to migrate thousands of existing contacts, integrate custom booking software, synchronise records with an accounting platform and implement specialised lead routing.

These projects require more than familiarity with a visual workflow editor.

They involve data modelling, integration architecture, testing, security and operational reliability.

The decision rule is straightforward: use native workflows for anything the platform already does well, and write custom code only where the platform's model runs out — multi-source routing, external system synchronisation, or reporting that spans several platforms. That distinction matters because unnecessary custom development can make a system harder to maintain, while forcing every process into native workflows can create an equally fragile architecture.

Even technically capable teams can run into problems when implementing CRM integrations.

Large workflows containing dozens of branches are difficult to test and maintain.

Smaller, clearly defined workflows are generally easier to troubleshoot.

An integration that works with ten contacts may behave very differently when processing thousands of records.

Developers should account for rate limits, batch processing, exponential backoff and retry behaviour.

Creating a new contact for every incoming event can quickly damage CRM data quality.

Contact matching, normalisation and carefully selected identifiers help reduce duplication.

Access tokens should never be exposed in client-side JavaScript or committed to public repositories.

Use appropriate secret management and grant integrations only the permissions they require.

Successful execution doesn't necessarily mean the system behaves correctly.

Test scenarios should cover missing information, duplicate events, failed API requests, appointment cancellations, opt-outs and unexpected responses from external services.

Observability matters too. Logs and alerts should help identify which step failed and whether the operation can be retried safely.

Not every automation problem needs an API integration.

A useful decision framework is to start with the simplest reliable implementation.

Standard appointment reminders — Native CRM workflows

Basic lead assignment — Native workflows where supported

External application synchronisation — API or integration middleware

Complex routing across several data sources — Custom logic with CRM integration

AI-assisted lead classification — AI service with validated outputs

Enterprise reporting across multiple platforms — Dedicated reporting or data pipeline

Hybrid architectures are often the most practical.

The CRM manages customer interactions and standard business processes, while specialised services handle requirements that benefit from custom code.

This separation can also make future maintenance more manageable.

CRM automation has become considerably more accessible, but ease of implementation shouldn't be confused with reliability.

A workflow that sends a message after a form submission is easy to demonstrate. A system that handles thousands of contacts, prevents duplication, respects communication preferences, synchronises multiple applications and recovers gracefully from failures requires more careful design.

GoHighLevel provides a useful foundation for combining customer management with automation, particularly when its native capabilities are supported by well-designed integrations.

For developers, the opportunity isn't simply to automate more tasks. It's to design systems that remain understandable, maintainable and reliable as businesses grow.

The strongest implementations aren't necessarily the ones with the most workflows, API connections or AI features.

They're the ones where the right information reaches the right system at the right time — and everything continues working when something inevitably goes wrong.

#10803. Twitch streamer HAchubby reveals type of cancer she’s been diagnosed with

Twitch streamer HAchubby has revealed the specific type of cancer she has been diagnosed with days after she first informed viewers that she had the disease.

The IRL streamer shared the update on X on November 8, confirming her exact diagnosis as B-cell lymphoma, a type of blood cancer, after traveling to Korea for treatment.

In her post, HAchubby said doctors have yet to confirm the stage of the cancer, but that it can be treated.

“My stage and outlook are still unclear, but treatment is available,” she wrote. “I’ll need more tests at the hospital.”

She added that the cancer can spread quickly, which is why she had to travel to Korea urgently.

My exact diagnosis is aggressive mature B-cell lymphoma. My stage and outlook are still unclear, but treatment is available.I’ll need more tests at the hospital. It’s a type of blood cancer. I was told that although it can spread very quickly which is why I had to come to…

The National Cancer Institute states that aggressive lymphoma “grows and spreads quickly, and has signs and symptoms that can be severe.”

B-cell lymphoma is a type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma, a cancer that starts in B-cells, the white blood cells that help the body fight infection.

HAchubby said she first felt pain in one specific spot around three years ago, but it would fade after a few days. She had planned to get a full checkup before moving to Austin but only managed some tests, which she now calls a big mistake.

Four months ago, around the time her father was diagnosed with leukemia, the pain became stronger and lasted for a full month.

She said several hospitals turned her away because she was paying out of pocket, and an urgent care doctor initially told her it was probably nothing serious.

Korean streamer HAchubby reveals what her family thinks of her Twitch career

Korean streamer explains how Twitch chat “ruined” her

Her new doctor then referred her for one type of scan, but the imaging center offered two, and fellow streamer QuQco, who was with her, insisted on both, a decision she credits with saving her life. When she had the scans a month later, it was the second one that showed something was wrong.

A doctor then told her it looked serious and recommended a biopsy that same day, which she agreed to on the spot despite the $750 out-of-pocket cost.

HAchubby has canceled her TwitchCon plans to focus on treatment, but has said she plans to keep streaming from Korea.

#10802. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

Materials provided by PNAS Nexus. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10800. Developer creates script to email Sam Altman about his unused RAM every day

A web developer has created a script that automatically emails OpenAI CEO Sam Altman to tell him how much RAM is sitting unused in their computer.

The project, appropriately named Ram-Altman, was published on GitHub by developer IainKay in response to Altman’s previous comments about the world’s limited supply of computer memory being wasted in personal PCs.

According to its GitHub page, Ram-Altman is a Bash script that monitors a Linux computer’s memory usage every five minutes.

If more than 10% of the machine’s RAM is available, the script can automatically send Altman an email detailing exactly how much memory isn’t being used, limited to one message per day.

The emails come with eight rotating subject lines, ten different messages, and six sign-offs to keep the correspondence varied.

One example, titled “You missed a spot,” reads: “Please don’t be alarmed, but there is 8.6 GiB of RAM on my desk that isn’t in a data centre. That’s 56.2% of what’s installed here, and as far as I can tell it has no plans today.”

The developer explains that the project was inspired by Altman’s appearance on the Cheeky Pint podcast, where he discussed the limited global supply of DRAM and the efficiency of running AI models in data centers.

“From a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer,” Altman said, adding that it was being used “maybe 5% of the time.”

The remarks attracted criticism, prompting Altman to clarify that he supports people having as much RAM as they want.

“I actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers,” he wrote on X.

but only because…i actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context.i have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time.

Altman also briefly changed his display name to “Ram Altman,” which inspired the project’s name.

Despite the joke, the developer has discouraged people from bombarding Altman’s inbox with messages.

“One email a day from one person is a joke. Ten thousand of them is a denial-of-service attack with better material,” the repository explains.

The script includes a dry-run option that lets users preview its emails without sending anything, and it doesn’t include Altman’s email address by default.
