#10798. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

Materials provided by The University of Hong Kong. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10796. How to Create Effective Cold Email Sequences That Generate Replies: A Developer's Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting with prospects via cold email isn't always straightforward, particularly when recipients receive dozens of messages every day. Even a relevant email can get lost in a crowded inbox. That's why many B2B companies use cold email sequences to maintain consistent communication and manage follow-ups.

However, creating effective email sequences involves more than writing messages and choosing sending intervals. Developers building or integrating email automation systems must also consider API reliability, message scheduling, data validation, email authentication, security, and workflow monitoring.

A well-designed email sequence combines relevant communication with reliable technical infrastructure. This article explains how to structure cold email sequences and explores the development practices that make automated email workflows more dependable.

A cold email sequence is a planned series of emails sent to prospective customers who have not previously established a direct relationship with the sender. These messages are delivered over a defined period, with each email designed to introduce an offer, provide additional context, address a potential concern, or encourage a response.

For example, a software development company might contact a growing SaaS business to discuss opportunities to improve its application infrastructure. The first email could introduce the company's expertise, the second could explain a relevant technical challenge, and the third could share a useful observation about application performance.

Each message supports the same overall objective while contributing something different.

From a developer's perspective, a cold email sequence can also be understood as an event-driven workflow. Every contact has a sequence state, scheduled messages, and conditions that determine whether the next message should be delivered.

A basic sequence might follow this structure:

This model is useful for developers working on CRM integrations, email platforms, customer engagement systems, and automated business workflows.

The first step is to establish a clear plan before writing any messages. Determine who you want to contact, what problem your offer solves, and what action you want recipients to take.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile, identifying relevant decision-makers, and researching their business needs. Next, select one primary campaign objective, such as generating positive replies, booking qualified meetings, or exploring potential partnerships.

Decide how many emails you will send, what each message will communicate, and how much time will pass between follow-ups. Make sure every email adds value instead of repeating information from previous messages.

For example, a software consultancy targeting B2B SaaS companies might introduce an application performance challenge in the first email, share a practical recommendation in the second, provide a relevant engineering case study in the third, and close the sequence with a polite final follow-up.

Before launching the campaign, review the prospect list, verify contact information, check email authentication, and test the complete sequence.

Developers should also establish technical requirements before implementation:

Define how contact information will be stored and validated.

Determine which events trigger scheduled emails.

Implement conditions that prevent unnecessary follow-ups.

Establish secure API connections between applications.

Record delivery results, failures, and recipient responses.

These considerations help transform a basic email campaign into a maintainable automation workflow.

Every cold email sequence needs a specific objective. Without one, messages may contain several unrelated offers or ask recipients to take different actions.

A SaaS company might want to schedule product demonstrations, while a software consultancy may want to connect with businesses planning infrastructure improvements. A recruitment firm might aim to establish relationships with technical hiring managers.

Your objective should influence the message and call to action.

For developers building campaign dashboards, goals should also be represented through measurable application events.

This example demonstrates how a dashboard might calculate a positive reply rate from stored campaign events.

In production, developers should also handle zero denominators, duplicate events, inconsistent reporting periods, and differences between accepted, delivered, and failed messages.

Effective cold email sequences begin with the right audience. Even a well-written email is unlikely to generate meaningful replies if it reaches people who have no interest in the subject.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile. Consider industry, company size, location, business model, growth stage, and the challenges your product or service addresses.

For example, a development consultancy offering cloud migration assistance might contact CTOs, engineering managers, and technical founders. Although these professionals may benefit from similar services, their priorities can differ.

A CTO may focus on infrastructure reliability and operational costs, while an engineering manager may prioritize deployment workflows, maintainability, and team productivity.

Segment prospects according to meaningful differences rather than sending identical messages to everyone.

Audience segmentation can be implemented through database queries, CRM filters, or API-based contact management.

A simplified contact object might look like this:

Structured data makes segmentation more predictable and helps prevent inconsistent personalization.

Contact data should be collected and processed lawfully, with appropriate access controls and retention policies.

Personalization helps prospects understand why you are contacting them specifically. However, effective personalization involves more than inserting a first name, company name, or job title into a template.

Research relevant professional information that connects the recipient's business to your offer.

For example, a development consultancy contacting a SaaS company could mention a recently announced application launch and explain how scalable deployment practices may support future growth.

The observation should be accurate and connected to a genuine technical challenge.

A practical personalization process involves three steps:

Identify a relevant professional observation.

Explain why it may matter to the recipient.

Connect it naturally to the proposed solution.

Developers can support this process with reusable templates and structured variables.

This simple example demonstrates template personalization.

In production, developers should validate template variables, prevent accidental disclosure of customer data, and escape untrusted values when generating HTML.

The subject line gives recipients an initial indication of what an email contains. A clear, relevant subject line helps them recognize the message's purpose.

Keep subject lines concise and connected to the recipient's business needs. Avoid exaggerated promises, artificial urgency, and misleading claims.

A question about your development workflow

Ideas for improving deployment reliability

A question about your engineering process

The best subject line depends on the audience and purpose.

Developers building email systems should also consider subject-line encoding, Unicode handling, template validation, and protection against header injection.

For example, email libraries should be responsible for constructing message headers rather than applications manually concatenating untrusted header strings.

SitePoint's tutorial on sending email using Node.js provides additional guidance on email construction and delivery.

The first email introduces your business and gives the prospect a reason to consider your offer.

A useful structure includes a personalized opening, a clear value proposition, a brief explanation of relevant expertise, and one simple call to action.

For example, a development consultancy contacting a SaaS business might explain that it helps software teams improve deployment workflows and application reliability.

Avoid introducing every service you offer or listing multiple benefits in the same email.

From a technical perspective, the first message should also establish the recipient's sequence state.

This state information allows a background worker to determine when an email is eligible for delivery.

Before sending, the worker must recheck the latest recipient status. A scheduled job should never override a newly recorded opt-out.

Subject: A question about your development workflow

I noticed [relevant observation about the company's software development process] and thought there might be an opportunity to improve deployment reliability.

Our team works with growing software companies on application infrastructure, development workflows, and performance improvements.

Is improving your development infrastructure a priority for your team this quarter?

The personalized observation should be specific and accurate. A concise message is more useful than a generic introduction filled with broad claims.

An effective cold email sequence follows a logical progression. Each message builds on the initial contact by introducing new information, clarifying value, or offering another way to engage.

Email sequencing tools can help teams schedule messages and manage follow-ups consistently. For developers integrating these systems into existing applications, it is important to understand how scheduling, contact states, reply events, and automation rules interact.

A five-email sequence can provide a practical starting point:

These intervals are starting points rather than universal rules. Adjust them to suit the audience, recipient preferences, and campaign performance.

For developers, scheduling is one of the most important technical aspects of email automation.

Sending messages directly inside an HTTP request can create unnecessary delays and make failure handling difficult.

A more reliable architecture uses background jobs.

This approach separates campaign management from email delivery.

For example, a worker can process scheduled messages independently while the main application continues handling user requests.

SitePoint's guide to implementing a task queue in Node.js explains how background processing can support workloads such as email delivery.

Production implementations should include persistent scheduling, retry policies, rate limiting, and idempotency controls to reduce duplicate messages.

Follow-up emails give prospects another opportunity to respond when they overlook an initial message or need additional information.

Each follow-up should introduce a useful detail, clarify the offer, or address a relevant concern.

For example, a software consultancy could use its first follow-up to explain a common deployment bottleneck. A later message might share a relevant case study or describe how the consultancy approaches application monitoring.

Subject: Re: A question about your development workflow

I wanted to follow up on my previous message.

One area worth reviewing is whether your deployment process includes automated testing, rollback procedures, and application performance monitoring.

These practices can help development teams identify problems earlier and improve release reliability.

Would a brief conversation about your current development workflow be useful?

This follow-up adds context without repeating the original message.

Stop the sequence when someone replies, opts out, or otherwise indicates that further contact is unwelcome.

Prospects receiving unsolicited emails may have little reason to trust an unfamiliar sender.

Relevant evidence helps establish credibility.

Social proof can include verified customer results, technical case studies, industry experience, or examples of previous work.

For example, a software consultancy might share a documented case study showing how automated testing reduced deployment failures.

When case studies are unavailable, demonstrate expertise through a practical observation or useful recommendation.

Developers building campaign systems should ensure that reusable case studies and message templates are versioned appropriately. This makes it easier to identify which content was sent and prevents outdated claims from being reused unintentionally.

Avoid exaggerated claims, invented statistics, and guarantees that cannot be supported.

A call to action tells the recipient what to do next.

In cold email outreach, a complicated request can discourage engagement because the prospect has not yet developed enough interest to commit time or resources.

Is this a priority for your team right now?

Would a short technical overview be useful?

Are you the right person to discuss this with?

Would you be open to exploring a potential partnership?

Choose a CTA that matches the purpose of the email.

Developers designing email templates should also consider accessibility. Links should use descriptive text, buttons should have sufficient contrast, and HTML emails should include readable plain-text alternatives.

Beyond content and scheduling, developers need to ensure that automated email workflows operate securely and consistently.

Email authentication helps receiving systems evaluate whether a message is authorized to use its sending domain.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework): Identifies servers authorized to send mail for a domain.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail): Uses cryptographic signatures to help verify message integrity and signing-domain responsibility.

DMARC (Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance): Defines how SPF or DKIM authentication should align with the visible From domain and provides reporting capabilities.

Developers responsible for email infrastructure should understand how these mechanisms work together.

SitePoint's article on email deliverability provides additional context about authentication, sender reputation, and delivery reliability.

Modern email workflows often integrate CRM systems, databases, automation platforms, and email service providers.

REST APIs allow developers to exchange information between these applications.

For example, an application might synchronize contact records, create campaigns, retrieve reply events, or update sequence states.

When building integrations, developers should:

Avoid exposing sensitive information in logs.

These practices improve maintainability and reduce the risk of inconsistent campaign data.

Webhooks allow external platforms to notify applications when events occur.

A simplified event handler could look like this:

This example illustrates application-level event handling rather than a particular provider's webhook format.

A production webhook endpoint must verify the provider's authentication or signature mechanism before processing events. It should also handle duplicate deliveries, out-of-order events, and temporary database failures.

Testing is essential when an application sends automated messages.

Messages are scheduled at the correct times.

Failed jobs do not create duplicate messages.

Templates render correctly across email clients.

API failures produce useful operational logs.

Tools such as Postman, Mailpit, and automated integration tests can help validate different parts of the workflow.

Use synthetic recipients and controlled test environments rather than sending test campaigns to real prospects.

Developers building campaign dashboards or CRM-connected applications may need to integrate an external sequencing platform rather than implement every sending feature themselves.

EmailBison is one example of a platform for managing cold email campaigns and follow-ups. Its API and integration capabilities make it relevant when evaluating how a third-party email system can fit into an existing application architecture.

For developers, the main consideration is not simply whether a platform can schedule messages, but how its capabilities interact with the application's data model and operational requirements.

For example, a CRM integration might follow this workflow:

This architecture introduces several engineering considerations.

Data synchronization: Contact records must remain consistent between the CRM and the email platform.

Error handling: API failures should be recorded and retried safely when appropriate.

Security: API credentials and contact information should be protected.

Observability: Developers should be able to investigate failed operations without exposing sensitive information.

The same architectural principles apply to other email automation providers. Before choosing an integration, review its current API documentation, authentication model, event coverage, rate limits, and data-handling requirements.

Several common mistakes can reduce the effectiveness and reliability of cold email outreach, even when businesses use automation.

Sending generic messages: Emails that could apply to any business fail to establish relevance. Use appropriate segmentation and accurate personalization.

Repeating the same pitch: Every follow-up should contribute something new instead of repeating the original message.

Making emails too long: Excessive explanations and multiple offers make it harder for prospects to identify the main point.

Using aggressive follow-ups: Sending messages too frequently can frustrate recipients. Choose reasonable intervals and respect recipient preferences.

Making unsupported claims: Exaggerated results and misleading promises damage credibility.

Ignoring recipient responses: Stop automated sequences when someone replies, opts out, or requests no further contact.

Failing to handle duplicate events: A webhook may be delivered more than once. Applications should use idempotent processing to avoid repeated actions.

Ignoring API failures: Temporary provider outages and rate limits should not silently corrupt campaign state.

Insufficient testing: Automation workflows should be validated with controlled test data before production deployment.

Scaling too quickly: Increasing sending volume before validating deliverability, infrastructure, and workflow reliability can amplify existing problems.

Avoiding these mistakes helps teams maintain a professional communication process while reducing operational risks.

A cold email sequence can start with three to five emails, including the initial message and follow-ups. The appropriate number depends on the audience, purpose, and recipient preferences.

Focus on providing useful information rather than increasing the number of messages unnecessarily.

Yes. Developers can integrate email sequencing platforms with web applications through APIs, webhooks, and background workers.

Common use cases include CRM synchronization, campaign dashboards, reply tracking, and workflow automation.

A practical starting point is two to four days before the first follow-up, followed by slightly longer intervals between later messages.

The schedule should account for recipient preferences and stop immediately when further contact is unwanted.

Yes. Sequencing tools can automate scheduling, follow-ups, contact management, and reply detection.

Human oversight remains important for message quality, data accuracy, security, and responding appropriately to interested prospects.

Testing should also verify that automated messages are not sent to suppressed contacts.

Yes. Small businesses may prioritize straightforward communication and immediate practical benefits, while enterprise prospects may require more information about security, integration, scalability, and stakeholder approval.

From a development perspective, enterprise integrations may also require stronger access controls, audit logging, and data synchronization.

Creating effective cold email sequences requires relevant messaging, thoughtful follow-ups, and reliable automation.

For developers, the underlying technical systems are just as important as the messages themselves. Contact validation, background scheduling, API integrations, email authentication, webhook processing, and error handling all contribute to dependable email workflows.

By treating email sequencing as a structured, event-driven application process rather than simply a series of scheduled messages, development teams can build systems that are easier to test, monitor, maintain, and extend.

The goal is not merely to send more emails. It is to create a reliable communication workflow that respects recipients and supports meaningful conversations.

#10794. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

#10792. Valorant team disqualified for cheating after knocking out Masters champion Derke

Riot Games has disqualified BONK from the VCT EMEA Kickoff Europe Qualifier after confirming one of its players used cheating software, just days after the team knocked out Derke’s Aurora Gaming.

BONK beat Aurora 2-1 in the Round of 32 of Qualifier 1 on November 6, before going on to reach the top four of the bracket.

In a statement on November 8, Riot said its anti-cheat team had been reviewing matches throughout the qualifiers, and that suspicious behavior involving BONK had been reported.

Competition Update: BONK, Kickoff Europe Qualifier #1 pic.twitter.com/gqnNRxOhiu

“Further investigation by our team confirmed that a participant on Team BONK was using unauthorised cheating software,” Riot wrote.

All BONK players and registered team members have been provisionally suspended from Riot-sanctioned competitions while the investigation into individual involvement continues, with final sanctions to be decided for each person.

Leo Faria, Riot’s global head of Valorant esports, didn’t hold back on X.

Aurora will face Project 100K in the Round of 16 on November 8, with the winner taking on YCATABLE in the quarter-finals for a place in the Qualifier playoffs.

#10790. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

#10787. How to Organize CSS with Cascade Layers in Large Web Projects

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

As a website grows, its stylesheet often becomes harder to maintain. A rule added to fix one component can unexpectedly affect another, and developers may find themselves adding increasingly specific selectors to override previous styles. Over time, these changes make CSS difficult to understand and update.

CSS cascade layers provide a way to organize styles into named groups and control their priority. Instead of relying entirely on selector specificity and the order of declarations, developers can establish a predictable structure for their stylesheets.

CSS determines which declarations apply by considering factors such as origin, importance, cascade layer priority, specificity, and source order. When several rules target the same element, understanding these factors helps explain why one style overrides another.

If all three rules apply to the same button under normal author styles, the ID selector wins because it has greater specificity.

This can become inconvenient when a project contains third-party styles, reusable components, and application-specific overrides. Developers may respond by adding more specific selectors, making future changes increasingly difficult.

Cascade layers offer another approach: establish a clear priority order before writing individual component rules.

A useful starting point is to divide styles according to their purpose. For example, a project might contain reset styles, third-party styles, reusable components, and page-specific adjustments.

You can establish these layers at the beginning of your stylesheet:

The declaration at the top establishes the order for normal declarations in these layers. For normal author styles, later layers have higher priority than earlier layers, regardless of selector specificity between those layers.

This means a simple selector in a higher-priority layer can override a more specific selector in a lower-priority layer.

For more background on building adaptable interfaces, developers can explore SitePoint's introduction to CSS container queries, which explains another way to structure maintainable component styles.

Not every project needs the same number of layers. A small website may require only a few, while a large application may benefit from a more detailed structure.

This is a suggested structure rather than a mandatory convention. The important part is defining the priority order intentionally and applying it consistently.

Cascade layers can help avoid selectors that become unnecessarily complicated.

For normal declarations, the second rule wins because overrides has higher layer priority, even though the first selector contains an ID and more class selectors.

This can make changes easier to review because developers do not need to keep increasing selector specificity to achieve the intended result.

However, layer order should not become an excuse to place every exception in a global override layer. Keep related styles close to their components and use overrides only when the exception is intentional.

Cascade layers have rules that developers need to understand before adopting them across an existing codebase.

For normal declarations, later layers have higher priority. For declarations marked !important, the layer priority is reversed. Important declarations in earlier layers take precedence over important declarations in later layers.

Unlayered normal author styles also take precedence over normal declarations inside author cascade layers. This matters when integrating a layered stylesheet with older CSS that remains outside the layer structure.

The MDN documentation on CSS cascade layers explains these priority rules and provides additional examples.

Before reorganizing a production stylesheet, test how existing third-party rules, inline styles, and important declarations interact with the new structure.

Rewriting an entire stylesheet at once can introduce regressions. A safer approach is to define the intended layer order, migrate one group of styles at a time, and compare the rendered interface after each change.

Start with styles that are easy to isolate, such as resets or a small set of reusable components. Check buttons, forms, navigation, and responsive layouts at several viewport sizes.

Browser developer tools can help identify which declaration is winning and whether it belongs to the expected layer. Visual regression tests are also useful when the application has many shared components.

For teams, documenting the layer order and agreeing on where new styles belong can prevent the stylesheet from becoming disorganized again.

CSS cascade layers provide developers with a practical way to manage style priority in growing applications. By separating resets, vendor styles, components, and utilities into a clear hierarchy, teams can reduce specificity conflicts and make stylesheets easier to maintain.

The best results come from a deliberate layer structure, consistent naming, and careful testing during migration. Cascade layers do not remove the need to understand CSS specificity, but they give developers another tool for controlling how styles interact.

#10785. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

#10783. Japanese pizza chain apologizes after TikTok AI tool creates unauthorized Dr. STONE ad

PIZZA-LA has apologized after an AI-powered TikTok advertising feature generated and published an unauthorized video featuring characters from Dr. STONE.

The Japanese pizza chain did have an official collaboration with the anime, which ran from October 1 through November 1 and included limited pizzas and merchandise featuring newly drawn artwork. However, the video served through PIZZA-LA’s official TikTok account had not been reviewed or approved by the Dr. STONE production committee.

The incident was first noticed after viewers spotted advertisements using character poses, facial expressions and artwork that differed from the official campaign visuals. Questions quickly spread online about whether the video had been created with generative AI and whether the production committee had approved it.

【TVアニメ「Dr.STONE」とのコラボレーションに関する広告配信についてのお詫び】多大なるご心配とご迷惑をおかけしましたことを、心よりお詫び申し上げます。https://t.co/5N2Y05L83S pic.twitter.com/yiLbuaMhhP

PIZZA-LA’s operator, Four Seeds, said the video was published because an AI-powered automatic ad generation and delivery feature had been left enabled when staff set up the TikTok campaign.

The company said it did not fully recognize that the feature was active and completed the advertising setup without disabling it. As a result, TikTok delivered a video related to the Dr. STONE collaboration despite the fact that it had not gone through the production committee’s prior supervision.

PIZZA-LA said the advertisement has been removed and that it has paused all advertising on TikTok while it reviews what happened.

The company also apologized directly to the Dr. STONE production committee and said it would reassess its advertising settings and content-management procedures to prevent a similar incident.

Four Seeds also apologized for the delay in announcing the issue, saying it needed time to confirm the facts. The company emphasized that anime works and characters reflect the efforts of their creators and production staff, and acknowledged that the unauthorized video damaged trust in the collaboration.

#10781. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

#10778. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10776. Alan Turing's biggest AI assumption may have been wrong

Alan Turing's famous ideas about artificial intelligence may have sent AI research down the wrong path for the past 75 years, according to prominent computer scientist Peter J. Denning.

In his new book, Turing's Mistake: Escaping the Yoke of Unintelligent Machines, Denning argues that two foundational assumptions made by Turing in 1950 continue to shape AI research today. The first is that intelligence can exist independently of a physical body and therefore be recreated in computer software. The second is that a machine can demonstrate intelligence by successfully imitating a human in conversation, an idea that later became known as the Turing test.

"These two claims have shaped much of AI research and development," Denning writes. "My premise is that our acquiescence to these claims has led to the AI mess in which we find ourselves today."

Denning argues that pursuing artificial general intelligence (AGI), or machines with human level intelligence, is unlikely to succeed. Instead, he warns, the technologies society is building could introduce significant new risks.

At the heart of Denning's argument is the idea of tacit knowledge, the vast amount of human understanding that cannot easily be put into words or represented in a form that computers can process.

He says machine learning cannot capture five major categories of tacit knowledge: common sense, everyday interactions with people and the environment, emotions and perception, practical performance skills, and the social and historical knowledge embedded in culture.

Researchers have long attempted to organize common sense into databases. One of the best known efforts was Douglas Lenat's Cyc project, which began in the 1980s with the goal of creating an extensive collection of common sense facts. After four decades of work, the project contained roughly 25 million entries.

"Yet even this treasury could not add up to a background of common sense sufficient to make expert systems smart enough to be experts," Denning notes. "Cyc validated that much of the knowledge that makes people experts cannot be articulated as propositions."

Denning believes practical skills present an even greater challenge.

"Our performance skills in thousands of domains cannot be communicated to machines," he explains. "Whereas descriptions of skillful outcomes ('know what') can often be represented as bits and stored in a machine, we do not know how to encode the embodied knowledge for skillful performance ('know how')."

He points to accomplished musicians as an example.

"A virtuoso violinist can play beautiful music yet cannot describe to an acolyte how to produce it.

"Even if a robot could observe and imitate skilled humans, having no biological body, a robot cannot grasp how the musician feels when playing beautiful music or how an audience feels when hearing it."

Denning also includes intuition, gut feelings, imagination, and spontaneous creativity among the forms of tacit knowledge that remain beyond the reach of machines.

Denning argues that all of these limitations stem from what he calls the "representation problem."

Computers can only perform calculations using data and instructions that have been encoded into physical forms they can recognize and process. Tacit knowledge, however, does not naturally fit into that framework.

"Behind every word is a deep well of tacit knowledge that gives it meaning," Denning says. "Words are but symbolic representations of meanings, not the meanings themselves. Commonly used Large Language Models, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini only manipulate words, they cannot know or understand the meaning of what they are saying."

According to Denning, this creates a fundamental divide. Because scientists still cannot fully explain how tacit knowledge works in humans, they also cannot translate it into a form machines can use.

"How we host tacit knowledge is largely a mystery," Denning admits. "All we know is that it is embodied. We have no idea what we might observe and measure in our bodies to reveal it."

Denning also argues that intelligence depends heavily on context, the surrounding circumstances that give words, actions, and decisions their meaning.

Context allows people to recognize sarcasm, humor, sincerity, and emotion. It helps determine when to be diplomatic, when to joke, and how to interpret countless social cues.

"When you inquire into where an assumption of the current context came from, you discover it rests on previous conversations from previous contexts. Each of those in turn rests on further previous conversations and their contexts. This pattern is endless and fractal," Denning explains.

Culture presents another major obstacle for AI.

Denning describes culture as encompassing values, norms, judgments, history, communities, moods, and even relationships involving power and care.

"Human conversations are imbued with background assumptions that give meaning and relevance to the words being used," Denning explains.

"Scaling up LLMs with ever larger neural networks will not enable them to acquire the embodied human knowledge we call culture. LLMs will not attain the objective of the Turing test: to demonstrate machine thought indistinguishable from human thought."

AI Safety and the Limits of Human Understanding

Denning concludes that humans and AI systems may ultimately develop different forms of tacit knowledge that neither can fully understand.

"Machines cannot read our tacit knowledge and we cannot read theirs," he writes. "We are aliens across an uncrossable divide."

He argues this gap raises serious concerns about AI safety. If machines cannot interpret the unspoken context behind human intentions, reliably aligning advanced AI systems with human goals may prove impossible.

"Through AI automation, agentic networks of machines are likely to develop their own machine intelligence that does not reach the level of human general intelligence but is still quite capable of creating severe problems for humans. This threat is a greater than a take-over by superintelligent machines," he explains.

"Machine intelligence has different concerns from us and does not appear to care about us. Its ways of thinking and problem-solving look alien to us. We do not yet know how to live safely with these machines.

"Pulling back from an AI automation singularity will demand much from us. We start by accepting that the familiar culture is fading away as intelligent machines appear in our society and we do not know what is coming. We decline to think like machines or be subservient to machines. We refuse to submit to a yoke imposed by low-intelligence machines. Most importantly, we reassert our humanity, declare once again what makes us different from machines, and celebrate those differences."

Materials provided by Taylor & Francis Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
